 SECTION 1.0
 INTRODUCTION

1.1 Background

 This report presents and discusses enhanced property-composition models that have been
developed for Hanford high-level waste (HLW) glasses. Glass property-composition models will
be used to control both the HLW and Low Activity Waste (LAW) vitrification process at the
Hanford Tank Waste Treatment and Immobilization Plant (WTP) to ensure that the glass meets
product quality and processability requirements. The quality and the validity ranges of the
property-composition models and the supporting database will greatly influence waste treatment
operations, including the waste loading that is achievable in the glass for each waste
composition. The current baseline models for operation of the WTP were developed under the
Bechtel National, Inc. (BNI) WTP contract [1-4]. These models were based on HLW and LAW
glass property data over a range of compositions that was guided primarily by the WTP contract
and the specific set of tanks specified therein. Enhanced models, which are described in the
present report, are required in order to take advantage of improvements in glass waste loadings
that have been achieved since the baseline models were developed and to address the full waste
inventory.

 The HLW testing under the BNI WTP contract, described in References [1-2], was
directed towards waste streams from the then-planned early feed tanks for the WTP (i.e.,
AZ-101, AZ-102, C-106/AY-102, and C-104/AY-101). While the baseline models may be
adequate for commissioning of the facility and processing of the specified tanks, refinements and
extensions or augmentations of these models will be required to process the balance of the tank
waste inventory. The overall WTP mission will require the immobilization of tank waste
compositions that are variously dominated by mixtures of aluminum, chromium, bismuth, iron,
phosphorous, zirconium, and sulfur compounds as waste-loading-limiting components. Further,
the WTP contract includes minimum waste loading requirements that are generally rather modest
while loadings significantly higher than those in the current baseline are likely achievable. In
order to achieve these higher waste loadings and to expand the applicable composition regions,
however, new models will be needed to support the optimized operation of the WTP.

 Since the development of the baseline WTP models, the Office of River Protection (ORP)
has directed a program of work to examine various approaches for optimization of the
performance of the WTP, including increasing the waste loadings in the LAW and HLW glass
products [5–21]. As a result, a significant amount of new data has been collected that can be used
for improvement of the baseline models. Compilation of the ORP HLW and LAW glass data for
this purpose was documented previously [22]. Expansion and enhancement of the HLW models
intended for use at the WTP is a multi-year effort that employs a phased approach. The first
phase employed a combined dataset including both WTP and ORP glasses to cover a larger
region of HLW glass compositions [23]. Both actively and statistically designed glasses were

 10

included in Phase 1. The present report describes the results from Phase 2 of that ongoing effort
to develop enhanced HLW property-composition models.

 The Phase 1 HLW models were developed for the following glass properties: Product
Consistency Test (PCT) responses for B-, Li-, and Na-releases, spinel crystallization (one-
percent crystal fraction temperature, T1%), melt viscosity, melt electrical conductivity, and
Toxicity Characteristic Leach Procedure (TCLP) Cd responses [23]. During the Phase 1 work,
testing was also performed to collect data on nepheline formation upon Canister Centerline
Cooling (CCC); however, development of a model to predict nepheline formation is the subject
of a separate ORP task. Phase 1 models were developed for the expanded composition region
including the higher waste loading ORP glass formulations. With the exception of the electrical
conductivity model, the regression and validation statistics for these models are comparable to or
slightly worse than those for the original WTP baseline models but the new models are valid
over much wider composition ranges. The Phase 1 viscosity and electrical conductivity models
showed comparable or slightly better performance when compared to the respective WTP
baseline models, in spite of a greatly expanded composition region [23].

 The above models are “global” models in the sense that each model uses a single model
form over the entire glass composition region of interest. However, as the compositional spaces
expand, it is possible that the performance of such models may worsen, yielding less accurate
property predictions performances. In such cases, it may be useful to also consider “local”
models, which may be more accurate since they cover smaller compositional regions.
Preliminary local models were developed in Phase 1 for sub-regions of low alumina (Al2O3 ≤
13.1 wt%) and high alumina (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) [23]. This choice of division was made over
other choices because (i) while the WTP glasses were developed primarily for iron-limited
wastes, many of the ORP glasses were formulated specifically for the aluminum- and the
aluminum/sodium-limited wastes, (ii) the maximum component limit for Al2O3 in the WTP
baseline model validity region is 13.1 wt%, and (iii) a design feature for the Phase 1 test glass
(high-alumina augmentation) matrix limits the Al2O3 content to a minimum of 13.1 wt%. The
Phase 1 low-alumina local models generally afforded slightly better performance than the global
models [23]. This was expected since there were very sizable overlaps in the data used in model
development for both kinds of models. Development of high-alumina local models, however,
was limited by the relative paucity of available data. Not only is the high alumina region covered
by fewer glasses, most of the high-alumina glasses were actively designed and therefore provide
uneven coverage.

 The present work represents Phase 2 of the HLW modeling efforts and closely follows
the Phase 1 approach. Specifically, the combined WTP/ORP property-composition data
(including those used in Phase 1 modeling) were reviewed with respect to coverage of desired
composition spaces. This review identified composition spaces that required augmentation,
where more data were needed to provide better coverage and to improve model predictions.
Statistical design was then used to develop two glass matrices to cover these composition spaces.
After ORP review and approval of the test matrices, crucible melts were prepared and glass
properties measured. In addition, HLW glass property-composition data that became available
from other ORP tasks that were performed since the Phase 1 data were compiled were added into
the Phase 2 data set. Subsequently, the HLW glass property-composition models were revised

 11

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

using the expanded dataset. It is expected that the Phase 2 models will be further revised and
improved as yet more data become available.

1.2 Objectives

 Development of new HLW property-composition models will be a multi-year task
consisting of the principal steps listed below. The focus is to extend and enhance the present
WTP models for PCT release, spinel crystallization (T1%), viscosity, electrical conductivity,
TCLP Cd response, and nepheline formation on Canister Centerline Cooling (CCC). Spinel
crystallization and nepheline formation on CCC are expected to be the most constraining with
respect to waste loading. The work described in this report is responsive to the applicable Test
Plan [24]. The objectives of this work were to:

  Incorporate property-composition data from new glasses into the database.

  Assess the expanded WTP-ORP database and HLW property-composition models.

  Identify composition spaces in the database that need augmentation.

  Develop statistically-designed composition matrices to cover the composition regions
 identified in the above analysis.

  Prepare crucible melts of glass compositions from the statistically-designed composition
 matrices and measure the properties of interest.

  Incorporate the above property-composition data of the matrix glasses into the database.

  Assess HLW models with extended datasets.

  Develop new enhanced HLW property-composition models.

 The models presented in this report will be revised periodically as new data become
 available from ongoing and planned efforts to continue to expand and improve the underlying
 data set.

 12

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

 SECTION 2.0
 IDENTIFICATION OF COMPOSITION GAPS AND
 DEVELOPMENT OF AUGMENTATION MATRICES

 Since the completion of Phase 1 to develop enhanced HLW glass property-composition
models [23], VSL has continued to develop a range of formulations for ORP that provide
improved waste loadings and/or glass production rates. The new glasses were focused on
selected waste oxide components (e.g., sulfur or zirconium) or on demonstration of the viability
of the WTP HLW direct feed strategy (see below). These glasses span compositional regions that
are well outside the valid composition regions for the baseline WTP property-composition
models. Additional work is required in a number of areas in order for the WTP to be able to take
full advantage of the benefits of these new glass formulations. This includes the revision and
extension of the current baseline WTP models and the compilation of appropriate datasets on
which to build enhanced models. Those datasets should include relevant existing data plus data
from testing designed to address gaps in the existing datasets. As indicated in the Test Plan for
this work [24], in view of the very large composition space needed to address the entire Hanford
inventory, multiple steps will be needed to develop a sufficiently extensive dataset to support
model development over that composition space. The present work represents the second phase
of a continuing development effort. It is anticipated that subsequent phases of development work
will be required to further revise and refine the HLW property-composition models.

 In order to develop test matrices to augment existing glass composition regions, a subset
of the WTP and ORP HLW glasses was selected as the basis for the ORP-Phase 2 statistical
design. This section provides a summary of the glass formulations developed since Phase 1 and
describes the development of the test matrices.

 The datasets used to develop models for HLW glass properties included all data collected
under the WTP project since 1998 that satisfies all applicable quality assurance (QA)
requirements. Glasses produced from HLW melter campaigns were not used directly for
modeling but the target glass compositions for all melter tests were previously tested in crucible
melts and those data were included in the modeling datasets. Most of the earlier modeling data
were from actively designed1 glass formulations that relied on glass science to develop glass
compositions with desired properties. Other data were from statistically designed composition
matrices to cover the HLW glass composition region or sub-region of interest. Data used for
HLW glass property modeling also included glasses selected to assess the performance of the
preliminary Immobilized High Level Waste (IHLW) formulation algorithm [25] and glasses
prepared from actual waste samples. Data from actual waste glass samples were relatively

1
  “Actively designed” refers to glasses developed to meet certain specific requirements such as glass composition to
treat a HLW tank waste stream that has to meet all product quality and processing requirements. In this approach,
information from characterization of one set of glasses is used to guide formulation of future glasses, with little or
no intent to cover a composition space. “Statistically designed,” on the other hand, refers to a set of glass
compositions designed using statistical experimental design methods to cover a composition space.

 13

limited. However, the compositions of the actual waste glasses were based on actively designed
glasses that were developed and tested at VSL using simulants; data from these simulant glasses
were incorporated into the database. The WTP modeling report and the Phase 1 report provide
detailed discussions of the target compositions and various data for these glasses [2, 23].
References [22] and [23] provide a description of the ORP glasses studied in Phase 1 and their
properties.

2.1 ORP-2014 HLW Glasses

 Since the completion of the Phase 1 modeling work [23], several HLW glass studies have
been completed at VSL. Glasses from those studies were all actively formulated. Data from those
studies have been incorporated into the database. However, those glasses were not included in
the statistical design of the test matrices in this work either because they were not available in
time or because they were removed during initial screening. They were, however, included in
updating of Phase 1 HLW models. To distinguish these glasses from those previously reported,
they will be designated as the ORP-2014 (actively designed) HLW glasses. This subsection
summarizes the sources of the new data and the objectives of the studies that supplied the data.
Table 2.1 lists these glass studies and the number of glasses added into the database.

Management of High Sulfur HLW (Reference [26])

 Tests were conducted at the crucible scale and on the DM10 melter to measure sulfur
solubility and to determine maximum feed sulfur concentrations that can be processed without
the formation of secondary sulfate phases for a projected high sulfur HLW stream. The Hanford
Tank Waste Operations Simulator (HTWOS) Model Run Batch 1925 was selected as the target
waste stream. Thirteen new HLW glass formulations were developed and tested before one was
selected as a target to support melter runs. The target formulation has a waste loading of 52 wt%
and a target sulfur concentration of 0.95 wt% SO3. During melter testing, the sulfur content of
the feed was progressively increased to 1.63 wt% SO3 (on a glass basis) and a measured SO3
content of 1.13 wt% in the glass product, without any observations of secondary sulfur phases.

Support for HLW Direct Feed (Reference [27])

 In the HLW direct feed option under consideration for early WTP operations, the
pretreatment facility would be bypassed in order to support an earlier start-up of the vitrification
facility. This work evaluated the direct feed strategy by developing new HLW glass formulations
for four waste blends from Hanford tank AY-102 with varying amounts of HLW and LAW.
Compositions of the waste blends were estimated assuming no pretreatment other than washing.
Waste Blend 1 assumed no washing, Blend 2 one wash cycle, Blend 3 two wash cycles, and
Blend 4 is the fully washed HLW solids. As the number of wash cycle increases, the contribution
of HLW to the overall waste composition increases.

 14

 Twenty-one glass formulations were developed and tested for the four AY-102 waste
blends on crucible scale. The waste loading limiting factors varied from K-3 refractory corrosion
(Blend 1) to spinel crystallization (Blends 3 and 4). A series of melter tests were performed on
the DM100-BL vitrification system for the four blends of simulated HLW AY-102 solids and
supernates with glass forming additives optimized for each blend.

High Waste Loading Glass Formulation Development for High-Sr HLW (Reference [28])

 This work focused on developing high waste loading glass formulations to treat a
representative high strontium Hanford HLW stream. A number of HLW batches to be processed
at the WTP will have SrO concentrations extending up to about 7 wt%. Additionally, since
strontium forms highly insoluble carbonates, which are stable at relatively high temperatures,
these wastes have the potential for carbonate decomposition in the cold cap, leading to foam
generation and low processing rates. Melter runs were also conducted to determine the effect of
the form of strontium in the waste on cold cap behavior and processing rates.

 Composition-property data for ten high strontium glasses from this work have been
incorporated into the HLW database. The concentrations of SrO in these glasses range from
3.84 wt% to 4.23 wt%. They also have moderately high concentrations of Al2O3 in actively
designed glasses (12.48 wt% to 13.73 wt%). Six of these glasses underwent CCC heat treatment
before PCT leach testing. As was the case with CCC glasses in WTP modeling [2], the 6 CCC
glasses were collected in the database as replicates.

High Waste Loading Glass Formulation Development for High-Zr HLW (Reference [29])

 Crucible scale tests were used to collect data on two sets of new HLW glass formulations
with high waste loadings to support melter testing, one for C-104/AY-101 waste and another for
Batch 773 waste from the Best Basis Inventory (BBI) HTWOS model run. Both wastes are high
in zirconium, with ZrO2 concentrations reaching > 35 wt%. The primary issue in formulating
glasses for both wastes was to limit crystallization of secondary phases, with baddeleyite (ZrO2)
being the major phase observed for both wastes. Spinel crystallization was also important in
glasses formulated for the C-104/AY-101 waste.

 A total of 19 new HLW glass formulations were developed and tested, 7 for the
C-104/AY-101 waste (HLW-ZrA series) and another 12 for the Batch 773 waste (HLW-Zr
series).

 The four glass formulation studies above supplied 70 ORP-2014 glasses (including 7
CCC samples that are considered replicates) to the HLW database. Table 2.2 summarizes the
compositions of these actively designed glasses. Five of these glasses have Al2O3 concentration
higher than 13.1 wt% (HLW-HSr-2, -7, -7CCC, -8, and AY102D2-02). A summary of the
property data collected on the ORP-2014 glasses is given in Section 4.

 15

2.2 Development of Augmentation Matrices

 A large portion of the ORP work at VSL has been directed at waste streams dominated by
aluminum and aluminum plus sodium because of the large fraction of the inventory that is
limited by aluminum. In that work, HLW glass compositions were successfully developed with
greatly improved waste loadings and compliant processing and performance characteristics [12,
13]. The ORP dataset therefore consisted of a much higher and wider compositional range for
Al2O3 when compared to the WTP dataset; the upper bound of Al2O3 that defines the
composition validity regions for most WTP HLW models is 13.1 wt% (8.6 wt% for TCLP
Cd-release model) but ORP HLW glasses with > 29 wt% Al2O3 have been developed. The
emphasis during Phase 1 was therefore centered on two aspects of the then-existing data in the
combined ORP and WTP database: (i) improving the coverage in the high aluminum
composition regions; and (ii) filling in some of the gaps between the two datasets. Two
augmentation matrices were designed and developed to address these needs [23]. The first Phase
1 matrix consisted of 20 glasses designed to augment high aluminum composition regions, which
were defined by the selection of glasses with > 13.1 wt% Al2O3 (and < 41 wt% SiO2). It is worth
noting that there had been no previous statistical design that had focused on the high aluminum
composition region (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). The second Phase 1 matrix was designed to include 30
glasses with the intent of bridging the gaps between WTP and ORP glasses.

 Development of high-alumina local models during Phase 1 was found to be limited by the
paucity of the available data. Not only is the high alumina region covered by fewer glasses, but
most of the high-alumina glasses were actively designed and therefore provide comparatively
uneven coverage. It was therefore decided that glass composition selection for testing in the
present phase would continue to support augmentation and improvement of the coverage in the
high aluminum regions. In order to adequately cover the compositional space of these regions,
the design was conducted in two parts: a high alumina (13.1 wt% < Al2O3 < 22.1 wt%) region
and a ultra-high alumina (Al2O3 ≥ 22.1 wt%) region.

 To develop the present test matrices, an approach similar to that employed in Phase 1
testing was followed. The approach consisted of selecting a subset of existing WTP and ORP
HLW glasses to provide the basis for the statistical design of augmentation matrices. The design
compositional space was selected so that the lower and upper bounds of the major glass
components include all of the augmentation glasses, which was a requirement for the
augmentation calculation. In some cases, these bounds were broadened based on information
from the HLW waste inventory compositions. A set of formulation constraints were then
constructed to limit the generation of design glasses with unreasonable properties. Radioactive
glasses were excluded from this phase of testing. In addition, no replicates were included in the
testing.

 An initial review of HLW glass composition-property data from the combined WTP and
ORP dataset was performed. This dataset was relatively large (392 ORP glasses plus 305 WTP
glasses) and included the statistically designed matrix glasses from Phase 1 [23] and the actively
designed glasses from the high sulfur HLW study [26]. These glasses were screened to remove
those with low Al2O3 content (Al2O3 < 13.1 wt%) and other compositionally extreme points.

 16

This process resulted in a dataset composed of 60 glasses in the high alumina region (13.1 wt% <
Al2O3 < 22.1 wt%) and 45 glasses in the ultra-high alumina region (Al2O3 ≥ 22.1 wt%). All 105
glasses in this dataset are ORP glasses since the maximum Al2O3 concentration in the WTP
dataset is 13.1 wt%. Moreover, all 45 glasses in the ultra-high alumina region were actively
designed. Glasses used to support the development of the composition matrices for testing are
listed in Appendix A.

 With the screened datasets as design bases, a 2-layer matrix design was developed for
each region by using the commercial software package Design Expert (V.8) with a D-optimal
mixture design [30]. The details of the design of the 45 new HLW glasses in the two alumina
concentration regions are listed in Table 2.3. As shown in Table 2.3, a set of 13 variables (10
single oxide variables and 3 composite variables) were used for the design of glasses in the
ultra-high alumina concentration region. Furthermore, 9 of the 13 variables were held constant
(four variables set equal to 0) for the design of the 15 inner layer formulations and, as a result of
the limits selected, no existing HLW glasses were included in the inner layer. The 15 new
ultra-high alumina inner layer glasses and 45 existing HLW glasses were then used for the
augmentation calculation of 7 additional ultra-high alumina glasses subject to the outer layer
constraints shown in Table 2.3. The grouped variables of the ultra-high alumina design are
defined in Table 2.4. Table 2.3 also lists several additional constraints intended to screen out
glasses with outlying properties in melt viscosity, electrical conductivity, spinel crystallization,
glass formation, and PCT releases. Preliminary Phase 1 glass composition-property models were
used to calculate predicted property values. Note that a PCT boron release constraint was
introduced to avoid design glasses with excessively low silica (plus alumina) concentrations. No
PCT release constraints were used in the Phase 1 design because it was repeatedly observed that
PCT releases were indirectly limited by other property constraints. Finally, a constraint was
included to impose limits on estimated calcium phosphate liquidus temperature by using a simple
linear model that was derived from the limited existing data [31]. The compositions of the
composite variables “Others 1,” “Others 2” and “Rest” are given in Table 2.4. It is noted that no
replicates were included in the design in order to maximize the number of new compositions
being tested. However, earlier matrix designs included multiple sets of replicates and their results
have been discussed previously [23].

 The compositions of the resulting inner layer and outer layer design glasses are given in
Table 2.5. Table 2.6 gives the compositions of “Others 1,” “Others 2,” and “Rest” in these
glasses. The 22 ultra-high alumina glasses constitute the HLW14-UH series. The distribution of
the HLW14-UH glasses in comparison to the existing ORP HLW glasses is shown in a matrix of
scatterplots in Figure 2.1. Selected binary scatterplots are given in Figure 2.2. It can be seen in
Figure 2.2 that considerable compositional gaps exist in the ultra-high aluminum region (e.g.,
Al2O3 vs. Fe2O3).

 In the design for the high alumina region, a set of 17 variables (14 single oxide variables
and 3 composite variables) was used. Only five single oxide variables (Al2O3, B2O3, Li2O, Na2O,
and SiO2) were allowed to vary in the design of the 15 inner layer formulations. As a result of the
selected limits, no existing HLW glasses were included in the inner layer. The 15 new high
alumina inner layer glasses and 60 existing HLW glasses were then used for the augmentation
calculation of 8 additional high alumina glasses subject to the outer layer constraints shown in

 17

Table 2.3. The grouped variables of the high alumina design are defined in Table 2.4. The
property constraints (Table 2.3) have slightly different upper limits when compared to those used
in the ultra-high alumina design. The resulting 23 high alumina glasses constitute the HLW14
series.

 The compositions of the HLW14- glasses are listed in Table 2.7. The compositions of
“Others 1,” “Others 2,” and “Rest” are given in Table 2.8. The distribution of the HLW14-
glasses in comparison to the existing ORP glasses being augmented is shown in a matrix of
scatterplots in Figure 2.3. Selected binary scatterplots are shown in Figure 2.2.

 SECTION 3.0
 EXPERIMENTAL METHODS

 The experimental procedures used in the preparation and characterization of the 45 new
simulated HLW glasses discussed in Section 2.4 are presented in this section. The following
subsections discuss the preparation of glass batches, crucible glass melting, glass composition
analysis, and test procedures for PCT, T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP, and
nepheline formation on canister centerline cooling (CCC).

3.1 Glass Batching and Preparation

 Glass preparation began with a batching sheet that provided information on the required
starting materials and their weights. The information included the chemicals needed,
identification of the chemicals according to vendors and catalog numbers with the associated
purity, and the amounts necessary to produce about 420 g of glass with the target composition.
Chemicals were weighed and batched according to the batching sheets. The information found in
the batching sheets, including actual weights of chemicals used and their associated purities, can
be used to calculate the composition of the glasses. The batching chemicals were determined
such that the calculated compositions equaled the target compositions given in Tables 2.5 and
2.7.

 A blender was used to mix and homogenize the starting materials before they were
loaded into platinum-gold crucibles (Pt-Au) that were engraved with individual identification
numbers. The crucible melts were prepared in a random order. After batching was completed, the
loaded platinum-gold crucibles were placed inside a Deltech DT-28 (or DT-29) furnace with a
Eurotherm 2404 temperature controller. The glasses were melted for 120 minutes at 1150°C.
Mixing of the melt was accomplished mechanically using a platinum stirrer, beginning 15
minutes after the furnace temperature reached the target and continuing for the next 90 minutes.
The molten glass was poured at the end of the melting period onto a graphite plate to cool.

3.2 Analyses of Glass Compositions

 The primary method used for glass composition analysis was X-Ray Fluorescence
Spectroscopy (XRF) on powdered glass samples. A PANalytical Axios mAX-Advanced
wavelength dispersive XRF spectrometer was used for this purpose. The XRF spectrometer was
calibrated over a range of glass compositions using standard reference materials traceable to the
National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), as well as waste glasses such as
Argonne National Laboratory – Low Activity Waste Reference Material (ANL-LRM) [32] and
DWPF – Environmental Assessment (DWPF-EA) glass [33]. XRF analysis provides data for
most glass components of interest, except lithium and boron, which are analyzed for selected

 19

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

glasses by Direct Current Plasma - Atomic Emission Spectroscopy (DCP-AES), as described
below.

 The HLW glass samples were also analyzed by DCP-AES after being subjected to
microwave-assisted total acid dissolution in Teflon vessels according to VSL standard operating
procedures. Twenty milliliters of a 1:5 mixture of concentrated HF:HNO3 was diluted to 50 ml
and used for the dissolution. This procedure is similar to the American Society of Testing and
Materials (ASTM) Test Method C1412-99, which also employs a mixture of concentrated HF
and HNO3 in microwave digestion of pulverized glass samples. However, supplemental use of
HCl/H3BO3 is not included in the VSL procedure because boron is normally one of the analytes.
The resulting solutions were analyzed by DCP-AES for all constituents except anionic species
such as sulfur and halogens. These results complement those obtained by XRF, specifically for
boron and lithium, and confirm the glass concentration of components that exceed the current
VSL upper evaluation limit for XRF.

3.3 Product Consistency Test

 The PCT was conducted using 4 g of crushed glass (100-200 mesh, 75-149 m) placed in
40 ml of test solution (de-ionized water) inside 304L stainless steel vessels. These test conditions
result in a ratio of the glass surface area to the solution volume of about 2000 m-1. The PCT tests
were performed at 90ºC for 7 days according to ASTM [34], in accordance with the current WTP
contract requirement [35]. All tests were conducted in triplicate, in parallel with the DWPF-EA
standard glass included in each test set. The leachates were sampled after 7 days. One milliliter
(ml) of sampled leachate was mixed with 20 ml of 1M HNO3 and the resulting solution was
analyzed by DCP-AES. Another 3 ml of sampled leachate was used for pH measurement.

 In addition to the leachate concentrations, it is convenient and conventional to also
consider the normalized leachate concentrations. The normalization is performed by dividing the
concentration measured in the leachate for any given component by its fraction in the glass.
Thus, the normalized concentration Ci of element i (in g/L) is calculated from the elemental
concentration ci measured in the leachate (in ppm) as:

 ci
 Ci  , (3.1)
 fi

where fi is the mass fraction of element i in the glass.

 The surface area of the glass sample tested and the volume of leachant used will also
affect the measured leachate concentrations and, therefore, a standard value of their ratio
(2000 m-1) is specified in the PCT method [34]. A further normalization for this effect is often
considered by dividing the normalized concentration by the ratio of the surface area of glass
exposed to the solution volume (S/V, in m-1). The normalized mass loss (in g/m2) is then
obtained from

 20

 Ci
 Li  , (3.2)
 (S /V )

where S/V is the ratio of the glass surface area to the volume of the leachant, which for the
standard PCT is nominally 2000 m-1. Assuming this value of S/V, if Ci is expressed in g/L, one
need only divide by two to obtain Li in g/m2. Specification 1 in the WTP contract [35] requires
that the normalized mass losses of PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na be below the respective values
for the DWPF-EA glass. The nominal values for normalized leachate concentrations from the
DWPF-EA glass are 16.695, 9.565, and 13.346 g/L for PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na,
respectively.

3.4 One-Percent Crystal Fraction Temperature (T1%)

 Glass samples (about 3 to 5 grams each) were melted in a covered platinum or platinum
alloy crucible at a temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour to establish a common starting point before
heat treatment for 70 hours at prescribed temperatures between 750°C and 1150°C. At the end of
the heat-treatment period, the samples were quenched by contacting the crucible with cold water.
The sample was then prepared (grinding to –18 mesh) for Scanning Electron Microscopy/Energy
Dispersive X-ray Spectroscopy (SEM/EDS) analysis to determine the volume fraction of crystalline
phases and for identification of the major crystalline phases; a JEOL Model JSM-5910LV equipped
with Oxford Instruments INCAEnergy 300 system was used for this purpose. The crystal fraction
(vol%) was generally obtained as the average of four (up to eight) viewing area counts from the
powdered samples. For each glass, a sufficient number of heat treatments were performed to obtain
non-zero crystallinity data for at least three temperatures in order to reasonably constrain the T1%
value. In a few cases, however, non-zero crystallinity data were available for fewer than three
temperatures. Effort was also made to bracket the T1% value so that it could be estimated by
interpolation rather than extrapolation.

 Alternatively, the heat-treated samples were analyzed by X-ray diffraction (XRD) using
whole pattern fitting. Powdered samples (–325 mesh) of heat-treated glasses were homogenized
with an internal standard (α-Al2O3) before X-ray diffraction patterns were obtained with a
Thermo-Scientific X’tra θ-θ XRD system. Diffraction patterns were analyzed using JADE9 (MDI,
Inc.) software with the Rietveld whole pattern fitting module to identify the crystalline phase(s) and
calculate the wt% of each phase. The wt% crystallinity data were converted to vol% with the known
densities of the phases and the measured densities of the HLW glasses.

 The T1% value for each HLW glass was obtained by linear regression of the heat-treatment
temperature (°C) as the dependent variable versus crystal fraction (vol%) as the independent
variable. The choice of vol% as the independent variable, which has larger measurement errors than
temperature, is contrary to the selection that would normally be made for regression. As discussed
during the WTP models development [2], this “inverse regression” approach has the advantage of
facilitating the calculation of the standard deviation of T1% value. The differences in the T1% values
estimated using either choice of independent variable were generally small.

 21

3.5 Electrical Conductivity

 The electrical conductivity (EC) of HLW glasses was determined by measuring the
impedance of the glass melt at temperatures around 950, 1050, 1150, and 1250ºC as a function of
alternating current frequency using a calibrated platinum-rhodium electrode probe attached to a
Hewlett-Packard model 4194A or an Agilent 4294A impedance analyzer. The collected
impedance data were analyzed to obtain the direct current EC. The probe (analyzer along with
the crucible to assure that the geometry is replicated) was calibrated and checked periodically
using NIST traceable standard reference materials.

 The current WTP requirement is that glass melt EC be in the range of 0.1 to
0.7 Siemens/cm at 1100 to 1200 ºC [36].

3.6 Viscosity

 The melt viscosity (η) of each HLW glass was measured using a Brookfield viscometer
with a platinum-rhodium spindle and crucible. The relative torque of a rotating spindle immersed
in molten glass was measured as a function of rotational velocity (revolutions per minute (RPM))
at temperatures around 950, 1050, 1150, and 1250ºC. The viscosity of the molten glass was then
calculated from the collected data of torque versus RPM. The equipment was calibrated using
viscosity standard oils and checked periodically using a NIST traceable standard reference glass.

 Per current WTP requirements, glass melts should satisfy the viscosity limits of 10 to 150
poise at 1100 ºC [36].

3.7 Toxicity Characteristic Leaching Procedure (TCLP)

 The TCLP was performed on glass samples according to Environmental Protection
Agency (EPA) SW-846 Method 1311. This test determines the leach resistance of crushed glass
(<3/8”) in a sodium acetate buffer solution after 18 hours at 22°C with constant end-over-end
agitation. A mass of about 100 g of glass is leached in 2 L of TCLP extraction solution,
according to the method for non-volatiles. The surface area to volume ratio for this test is about
20 m-1, which is about two orders of magnitude lower than that in the PCT. The leachate
concentrations were measured by DCP-AES.

3.8 Nepheline Formation on Canister Centerline Cooling (CCC)

 Glass samples that underwent CCC heat treatment were distinguished from the original
glass samples by adding the extension “CCC” to the sample identifications. The CCC
temperature profile was provided by the WTP project [37]. Table 3.1 reproduces the HLW CCC
temperature profile and Figure 3.1 gives a graphical representation. As is the case with
isothermal heat treatment, the glass samples in platinum or platinum alloy crucibles were

 22

maintained at a “pre-melt” temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour before initiation of the CCC
treatment. At the end of the heat treatment, the samples were recovered and processed for
characterization by either SEM/EDS or XRD (see Section 3.4).

 23

 SECTION 4.0
 HLW GLASS DATA

 As summarized in Section 2, the current phase of database enhancement incorporated an
additional 115 HLW glasses, including 70 actively designed glasses (including 7 CCC replicates)
and another 45 that were statistically designed. These glasses are collectively referred to as
ORP-2014 glasses. Table 4.1 lists these ORP-2014 glasses and the property data collected for
them. Table 4.1 shows that only a subset of the actively designed glasses have been characterized
with respect to the selected properties; 55 actively designed glasses and replicates have measured
data for at least one of the following properties: PCT, electrical conductivity, viscosity, spinel
T1%, TCLP Cd-release, and nepheline formation. In contrast, efforts were made to collect data on
all of the properties for each of the 45 matrix glasses. Thus, data are available for most of the
listed properties for all of the matrix glasses; the primary exception is for spinel T1% either
because the primary crystalline phase was not spinel or because no crystallization was observed.
Overall, the ORP-2014 dataset comprises 100 (55 actively designed and 45 statistically
designed) glasses that have at least one property characterized. This section presents and
discusses the PCT, T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP Cd-release, and nepheline
formation results.

 Table 4.2 compares the number of glasses that were collected in each of the HLW glass
datasets (WTP [2], ORP-2012 [22], ORP-2013 [23], ORP-2014) according to the property of
interest. The latest combined ORP dataset comprises 494 glasses, which can be compared with
305 glasses in the WTP dataset. It should be emphasized, however, that the ORP dataset cover a
much wider compositional range (for example, recall that the maximum Al2O3 concentration in
the WTP dataset is 13.1 wt%, while it is 29.6 wt% for the ORP dataset). There are now 392
glasses with data for at least one of the properties of interest, compared with 285 glasses for the
WTP data set.

4.1 Product Consistency Test (PCT) Results

 Of the 63 actively designed ORP-2014 glasses (70 including CCC replicates, see Section
2.1), Table 4.1 shows that 34 glasses have data on PCT-boron (PCT-B) and PCT-sodium
(PCT-Na) releases while 28 have data on PCT-lithium (PCT-Li) releases either because these
glasses do not contain Li2O or the PCT release was below detection limit. By contrast, all of the
statistically designed ORP-2014 glasses have PCT data; 45 glasses have PCT-B and PCT-Na
data and 41 glasses have PCT-Li data (3 glasses do not contain Li2O and 1 has PCT-Li release
below detection limit). Table 4.3 presents the PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na releases for the 79
ORP-2014 glasses. Normalized PCT concentrations (g/L) and mass losses (g/m2) calculated
using target mass fractions of boron, lithium, and sodium in glass per Equations (3.1) and (3.2)
are also given in Table 4.3. The normalized PCT-B concentrations for the 79 ORP-2014 glasses
vary from 0.130 g/L to 10.291 g/L, PCT-Li concentrations vary from 0.152 g/L to 8.332 g/L, and

 24

PCT-Na concentrations vary from 0.079 g/L to 5.892 g/L. These ranges can be compared to the
WTP contract limits set by the DWPF-EA glass of:

                                       PCT-B: 16.695 g/L
                                       PCT-Li: 9.565 g/L
                                      PCT-Na: 13.346 g/L.

All ORP-2014 glasses therefore meet the respective WTP PCT contract limits.

 Figure 4.1 shows the ranges and median values of normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and
PCT-Na releases (g/L) measured on the 79 ORP-2014 glasses. Figure 4.1 also includes the
results obtained for the WTP glasses, the ORP-2012 glasses, and the ORP-2013 glasses for
comparison. The distributions of PCT releases are generally comparable among the various glass
sets, with the WTP glasses showing a wider range, partly because the WTP dataset consists of
considerably more statistically designed glasses. The combined WTP-ORP dataset now consists
of 524 HLW glasses with PCT-B and PCT-Na data (484 with PCT-Li data), but only two glasses
(one each from the WTP and ORP-2012 subsets) have PCT-B and PCT-Li releases greater than
the respective limits; no glass has a PCT-Na release above the sodium limit.

 The distribution of the normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na releases is displayed in
Figure 4.2 for the 79 ORP-2014 glasses. Similar to the Phase 1 finding, the distribution is
dominated by low PCT releases and there is a general paucity of data with high PCT releases
(e.g., PCT-B, Li, and Na releases > 5 g/L), as is the case with PCT data of existing HLW glasses.
Consequently, there is a relative lack of data at high PCT releases for model development, as was
noted previously [23].

 Figure 4.3 displays a plot of normalized PCT-Na and -Li releases versus normalized
PCT-B releases for the ORP-2014 glasses. It can be seen that for glasses with relatively low
leaching, the measured sodium (or lithium) and boron releases are generally congruent, as
previously observed with the ORP-2012 and ORP-2013 glasses. The concentration of boron is
often used to provide a measure of the extent of the reaction of the glass with the leachant, since
boron forms few secondary phases that precipitate from the PCT leachate. As leaching increases,
lithium and sodium dissolution behavior deviates from congruency with boron releases, which
may be due to the formation of lithium- and sodium-containing phases. The general effects of
alkalis on PCT boron releases are shown in Figure 4.4. While it is expected that increases in
alkali content would result in increased PCT releases, the data in Figure 4.4 show significant
scatter as a result of the effects of the many other glass components that are varying.

 The PCT leachate pH is also a good indicator of the extent of leaching. Release of alkali
ions from the glass matrix increases the leachate pH, which in turn increases the hydrolysis rate
of the silicate network. Figure 4.5 correlates the measured leachate pH with the amounts of alkali
oxides in the ORP-2014 glasses. Compared to Figure 4.4 the expected trend of increasing pH
with alkali oxides is more apparent.

 The effect of alumina on PCT responses has been reported to be non-linear and at low
concentrations, alumina improves glass durability with respect to PCT, but levels off at

 25

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

intermediate concentrations before decreasing the durability at the highest concentrations [38-
42]. Since the Phase 2 glasses were designed to contain up to 29.6 wt% Al2O3, the PCT data
were examined to determine whether such an effect was apparent. In Figures 4.6 and 4.7, the
normalized boron releases are plotted against Al2O3 and Al2O3+SiO2+ZrO2, respectively. No
clear trends are evident suggesting that if such an effect is present, it is obscured by the effects of
other glass composition variables.

4.2 One-Percent Crystal Fraction Temperature (T1%) Results

 Heat treatment of HLW glasses was performed between 700°C and 1150°C, at regular
temperature intervals of 50°C. The measured crystal vol% data and major crystalline phase(s) for
the statistically designed ORP-2014 glasses are shown in Table 4.4. Heat treatment data for the
actively designed ORP-2014 glasses have been reported previously [26-29]. Glass formulation
work for high-Zr HLW showed that waste loadings in the actively designed high ZrO2 glasses
were limited mainly by the crystallization of baddeleyite (ZrO2) and that spinel formation was
not important [29]. These glasses supplied no spinel T1% data to the ORP-2014 dataset (see Table
4.1). In contrast, for the other 32 actively designed ORP-2014 glasses, spinel was the
predominant crystalline phase and was observed in heat-treated samples of all of the glasses.
Other crystalline phases observed in these glasses included apatite, nepheline, and baddeleyite.

 Table 4.4 shows that an unusually large number of glasses did not show significant
crystallinity after heat treatment. For example, the glasses HLW14-UH01 through -UH11
showed only trace amounts or no secondary phases after heat treatments at temperatures between
700°C and 900°C. This was somewhat unexpected because the crystallization limits adopted in
the matrix designs were not particularly constraining. As a result, no T1% was estimated for these
glasses and they did not expand the spinel T1% database. For glasses that showed formation of
crystalline phases upon heat treatment, spinel was the primary phase, although very often it was
accompanied by other phases, including apatite and nepheline. All of these glasses were
collected into the spinel T1% dataset since previous modeling efforts also included glasses with
other crystalline phase(s) in addition to spinel [2, 23]. Apatite was the primary or sole crystalline
phase in six matrix glasses; these glasses did not contribute to the spinel T1% database but will be
valuable to support the development of a model for prediction of phosphate crystallization in
future work.

 To provide estimates of T1% values for the ORP-2014 glasses, linear regression of the
heat-treatment data was performed in a similar fashion to that in earlier work [23], fitting the
temperature and vol% crystallinity data to an equation of the form

 T = ao + a1 v ,

where v is the vol% crystallinity at temperature T (°C), and ao and a1 are the fitted intercept and
slope. The typical correlation observed between crystal vol% and heat treatment temperature is
relatively simple and can be adequately described by the linear equation above. In quite a few
glasses, however, the appearance of a second (or more) crystalline phase results in an abrupt
change of the linear relationship. Instead of the total vol% crystallinity, the T1% values in those

 26

cases are estimated based on the linear trend defined by the crystallization of the primary
phase(s). Table 4.5 summarizes the fitted intercepts and slopes for the ORP-2104 glasses, as well
as the estimated T1% values and the number of data points used in the regression.

 Overall, T1% was estimated for 76 ORP-2014 glasses. Regression was not performed for
16 matrix glasses because of insufficient crystallization data, as described above. Of the 76
glasses for which T1% was estimated, 52 precipitated spinel as the primary crystalline phase upon
heat treatment at temperatures close to T1%, only 20 of which were statistically designed. It
should be noted that the spinel T1% values estimated for the 32 actively designed glasses show a
distribution that is relatively uneven and centered around 950°C. The estimated spinel T1% values
span a range of 764°C to 1173°C, with a median of 936.0°C, which can be compared with the
WPT processing requirement of T1% ≤ 950°C. The spinel T1% values for ORP-2013 glasses range
from 734°C to 1409°C (HLW13A-18 is an extreme outlier), with a median of 986.9°C.
Figure 4.8 shows a histogram of the distribution of the estimated spinel T1% values for the 52
ORP-2014 glasses. That distribution changes significantly if only statistically designed glasses
(20 glasses) are included (Figure 4.9) and the median value decreases to 871.6°C. When
compared with matrix glasses from earlier studies [2, 23], the HLW14 and HLW14-UH glasses
show a much reduced propensity for formation of secondary phases.

 For the 24 glasses that had non-spinel primary crystalline phases, the estimated T1%
covered a temperature range of 681°C to 1309°C. The principal crystalline phases were
baddeleyite (ZrO2) and nepheline. A non-iron phase with a spinel structure (e.g., Mg/CaAl2O4)
was found in HLW14-23 and HLW14-UH18.

4.3 Electrical Conductivity Results

 Of the 115 ORP-2014 glasses discussed in Section 2, 70 have electrical conductivity (EC)
results, as summarized in Table 4.1. The combined WTP and ORP database now consists of 452
HLW glasses with EC data (Table 4.2). The EC versus temperature data are given in Table 4.6
for the 70 ORP-2014 glasses.

 The EC versus temperature data in Table 4.6 vary from 0.033 S/cm (HLW14-UH20 at
979°C) to 1.049 S/cm (AY102D1-04 at 1255°C). These values can be compared with those from
the ORP-2013 dataset (0.014 to 0.646 S/cm for 66 glasses), those from the ORP-2012 dataset
(0.004 to 0.812 S/cm for 106 glasses), and those from WTP dataset (0.034 to 1.376 S/cm for 210
glasses). The WTP requirement for HLW glass melt EC is that it be within 0.1 to 0.7 S/cm in the
temperature range of 1100 to 1200 °C. For measurements taken in this temperature range, the EC
results are all within the acceptable range except for four glasses (AY102D1-03, AY102D1-04,
HLW-Zr-02, and HLW14-UH20). Three of these glasses have measured EC values above the
0.7 S/cm limit. They are actively designed glasses that contain uncommonly high concentrations
of (Li2O + Na2O): the AY102D1- glasses support testing of the direct feed HLW option with no
pretreatment of HLW (only in-tank washing and settling) which can leave a large proportion of
LAW in the waste (resulting in > 22 wt% Na2O in glass), while high levels of alkalis are added
in the HLW-Zr-02 to suppress crystallization of ZrO2 [27, 29]. The EC result for the glass

 27

HLW14-UH20 is slightly beneath the 0.1 S/cm limit (0.093 S/cm at 1167°C); it contains only
7.60 wt% of Li2O + Na2O.

 Figure 4.10 displays the distribution of temperature values at which data were collected
for the ORP-2014 glasses with EC data. It shows that EC was measured at fairly consistent
temperatures which are moderately (10 to 30°C) higher than the four nominal values (950, 1050,
1150, and 1250°C) for most glasses. A few glasses (e.g., HLW14-17 and HLW14-UH14) have
noticeably lower measurement temperatures. As discussed previously, any impact of this
variation in measurement temperatures on model development, however, is expected to be small
[2, 23]. Figure 4.11 displays the EC data as a function of measurement temperature.

4.4 Viscosity Results

 Of the 115 ORP-2014 glasses discussed in Section 2, 70 were characterized for melt
viscosity. During viscosity measurement for four of these glasses (HLW14-20, -22, -23, and
HLW14-UH16), very unstable and non-Newtonian behavior was observed, especially at lower
measurement temperatures when crystallization was apparent. Viscosity data could be collected
only at fewer than four temperatures for these glasses and these data were not added to the
database since glasses with fewer than four viscosity data points were excluded from previous
model development [2]. As a result, only 66 ORP-2014 glasses have viscosity results, as
summarized in Table 4.1. The combined WTP and ORP database now consists of 430 HLW
glasses with viscosity data (Table 4.2).

 Table 4.7 gives the 264 viscosity versus temperature data points for the 66 ORP-2014
glasses. The viscosity data in Table 4.7 vary from 7.4 P (HLW-HSr-7 at 1256°C) to 2223.7 P
(HLW14-UH22 at 954°C). These can be compared to the viscosity ranges measured for the WTP
dataset (3.6 to 3166 P for 210 glasses), the ORP-2012 dataset (4.0 to 4617 P for 101 glasses), and
the ORP-2013 dataset (7.4 to 3050 P for 53 glasses) [2, 22, 23]. Five ORP-2014 glasses
(HLW14-22, -UH18, -UH19, -UH20, and -UH22) have viscosities exceeding the recommended
WTP limit of 150 P at 1100C [36], with interpolated values in the range of 180 to 325 P. All
actively designed glasses have interpolated viscosity values beneath the WTP limit. In addition,
there are no glasses with viscosities beneath the lower processing limit of 10 P at 1100°C.

 Figure 4.12 shows the distribution of temperature values at which measurements were
made for the ORP-2014 glasses with viscosity data. It shows that, in contrast to the EC
measurements, viscosity was measured at fairly consistent temperatures which are not too far
from the four nominal values (950, 1050, 1150, and 1250°C) for most glasses. As discussed
previously, any impact of this variation in measurement temperatures on model development is
expected to be small [2, 23]. Figure 4.13 displays the viscosity data as a function of measurement
temperature.

Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

4.5 TCLP Cd-Release Results

 Of the 113 ORP-2014 glasses discussed in Section 2, only 39 matrix glasses contain CdO
as a glass component, covering a CdO concentration range of 0.004 to 0.460 wt%. All of these
glasses underwent TCLP testing for Cd-release. Table 4.8 gives the measured TCLP Cd-release
data (cCd, mg/L) for 22 HLW14 and HLW14-UH glasses; it also gives the normalized Cd-release
data (rCd, g/L) according to the equation:

 c Cd (mg / L)
             rCd ( g / L)                                                                  ,
                              [1000(mg / g )][mCdO (g CdO / g glass)][0.8754(g Cd / g CdO)]

where mCdO is the mass fraction of CdO in glass. Both cCd and rCd were considered in developing
the different TCLP Cd-release models [2].

 As seen in Table 4.8, 12 glasses have Cd-releases below the detection limit. The
non-detect glasses were excluded from the dataset, leaving only 27 ORP-2014 glasses with
TCLP Cd-release data (Table 4.1) These ORP-2014 glasses have cCd and rCd values that cover
the ranges of, respectively, (0.030-0.370) mg/L and (0.017-0.120) g/L. The range of
unnormalized Cd-releases for the ORP-2014 glasses is much narrower than that of the WTP and
ORP-2013 glasses [23]. This is mostly a consequence of the lower CdO concentrations in the
ORP-2014 dataset. The target CdO concentration range in the ORP-2013 glasses, for instance,
was 0.006 to 0.970 wt%.

 During the development of the WTP TCLP Cd-release models, relatively few glasses in
the modeling set had Cd-releases above the delisting limit of 0.48 mg/L [2]. Further, the WTP
baseline model showed good prediction performance only up to about 0.25 mg/L but tended to
under-predict for larger Cd-releases. Since it is desirable to have glasses that span a wide range
of TCLP releases, it was recommended that data be collected to improve the performance of the
TCLP Cd-release model with Cd-releases near and beyond the delisting limit. Data collected
during the Phase 1 modeling effort resulted in 14 ORP-2013 glasses with Cd-releases above
0.48 mg/L. However, the data for the ORP-2014 glasses are all below the delisting limit.

4.6 Nepheline Formation Results

 The development work for the baseline WTP models did not include a model to limit the
formation of nepheline upon canister centerline cooling. Instead it was assumed that the
approach employed at the Defense Waste Processing Facility (DWPF) to control nepheline
formation would be applicable to the WTP HLW glasses. The nepheline discriminator (NSi),
which was developed by Li e al. [43, 44], is used as a process control constraint at DWPF and
relates the concentrations of Al2O3, Na2O, and SiO2 in glass as follows:

 x SiO2
                                     N Si                                 0.62 ,
 x Al2O3  x Na2O  x SiO2

 29

where xi is the mass fraction of component i. Glasses with NSi > 0.62 are predicted to be free
from nepheline upon canister centerline cooling.

 Findings from the Phase 1 modeling effort and many other studies have repeatedly shown
that the NSi is highly conservative, particularly with respect to high-aluminum wastes [12, 13, 16,
23, 45]. It can be seen above that NSi does not include the effects of all glass oxides on the
propensity for nepheline formation (e.g, B2O3 and CaO). While NSi is effective in screening out
glasses that form nepheline, it also screens out many compositions that do not, which is an
important limitation on the ability to achieve high waste loadings for high-aluminum waste
streams. The need for an improved nepheline discriminator is addressed in a different ORP task.

 To support the development of a property-composition model to constrain nepheline
formation, the Phase 1 report documented the collection of crystallinity data upon CCC heat
treatment for 211 HLW glasses, including 35 ORP-2012 glasses tested at Pacific Northwest
National Laboratory (PNNL) [46]. Table 4.9 presents the new nepheline formation data collected
for 52 ORP-2014 glasses, as summarized in Table 4.1. The data in Table 4.9 include glass
identifications, estimated total vol% crystallinity upon CCC heat treatment, identity of the major
crystalline phase(s) and, when applicable and available, vol% of nepheline. In a few cases,
nepheline-like aluminosilicate phases (e.g., eucryptite (LiAlSiO4)) were found in heat-treated
glass samples. Although these phases may also have adverse effects on the PCT releases of
glasses subjected to CCC, they are not considered as nepheline in the database; however,
constraints to limit the formation of nepheline-like aluminosilicate phases should also be
considered. The combined WTP/ORP database contains 263 glasses with data on nepheline
formation, including 49 statistically designed glasses (Table 4.2).

 Figure 4.14 relates the NSi values of ORP-2014 glasses to the vol% nepheline precipitated
during CCC heat treatment. Of the 52 ORP-2014 glasses with nepheline data, all but 2 have a NSi
value less than 0.62. The nepheline discriminator correctly predicted that nepheline would not
form in the 2 glasses with a NSi value larger than 0.62, but nepheline was not found in 36 of the
50 glasses with a NSi value less than 0.62. Figure 4.14 further demonstrates the undue
conservatism of the nepheline discriminator.

 30

 SECTION 5.0
 HLW PROPERTY-COMPOSITION MODELS

 This section documents the revision of the interim HLW property-composition models
for predicting various HLW glass properties using the updated combined WTP, ORP-2012,
ORP-2013, and ORP-2014 datasets. There are now 393 glasses in the ORP dataset with data for
at least one of the properties of interest as compared to 285 glasses for the WTP dataset. Data
collected on ORP-2014 glasses for PCT, spinel T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP
Cd-release, and nepheline formation are presented in Section 4. Preliminary models were
developed during Phase 1 of this work for PCT-B, PCT-Li, PCT-Na, T1%, melt viscosity, melt
electrical conductivity, and Cd-TCLP [23]; models for nepheline formation were not addressed
because that is the subject of a separate task. In addition to global models that made use of a
single model form over the entire glass composition region of interest, the Phase 1 efforts
included attempts to explore the development of local models, which were developed using only
a subset of the combined WTP/ORP data [23]. Investigation of the local modeling approach was
suggested during development of the WTP baseline models to address the possibility that non-
linear blending effects of HLW glass components on various properties (including PCT releases,
spinel T1%, and Cd-TCLP) may be more complicated than can be represented using a single
quadratic or cubic model [2]. This is especially true with the greatly expanded glass
compositional regions afforded by the ORP dataset. The approach adopted in Phase 1 in
exploring the development of local models involved dividing the WTP/ORP dataset into two
subsets: high-alumina glasses (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and low-alumina glasses (≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3).
This choice of division was made over other choices because: (i) while the WTP glasses were
developed primarily for iron-limited wastes, many of the ORP glasses were formulated
specifically for the aluminum- and the aluminum/sodium-limited wastes; (ii) the maximum
component limit for Al2O3 in the WTP baseline model validity region is 13.1 wt%; and (iii) a
design feature for the Phase 1 test glass (high-alumina augmentation) matrix limits the Al2O3
content to a minimum of 13.1 wt%. Development of high-alumina local models was found to be
restricted by the very limited amounts of data available [23].

 The present work focused on the design of test matrices, fabrication of test glasses,
collection and assessment of data to augment the high-alumina glass regions (all matrix glasses
were designed with Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%), and modeling work to update the Phase 1 models using
the combined WTP/ORP dataset. The updated models are referred to as the Phase 2 models; it is
expected that they will be revised and improved as more data become available, including those
from future statistical designs to cover the identified compositional gaps. Updating the property-
composition models followed the same approach as in Phase 1 [23]. In particular, new model
forms and different compositional sub-regions were not investigated for this revision. It is
expected that these areas will be revisited as more substantial additions to the dataset are made.

 Table 5.1 provides a summary of the number of HLW glasses available to support model
revisions, including division of the glasses according to aluminum concentrations into Group 1

 31

(Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). As noted during Phase 1 [23], even after
collecting ORP-2013 glass data to augment the high-alumina sub-region, Group 1 glasses
provide much better coverage for the low-alumina sub-region than do the Group 2 glasses for the
high-alumina sub-region, not only because there are more Group 1 glasses, but also because the
sub-region was the subject of more than five statistical matrix designs. This continues to be true
with the addition of the ORP-2014 data, as seen in Table 5.1. Specifically, the ORP-2014 glass
set provides only slightly more data to Group 2 than to Group 1 for most properties (e.g., 32
Group 1 glasses and 47 Group 2 glasses for PCT-boron release). In the case of spinel T1%, more
ORP-2014 data are available for Group 1 glasses than Group 2 glasses (see Section 4.2).

 In the following subsections, the results from model revisions using the combined dataset
for each property are presented and discussed. Summaries of the modeling parameters and the
performance of the various models that were studied for each property are compiled in tables in
each subsection. The statistical terms that are used in modeling are described in Appendix C of
the WTP baseline model report [2]. They are reproduced below for convenience.

 n = the number of data points used to fit the model,

 p = the number of parameters (coefficients) in the model form estimated via regression on
 the data,

 yi = the measured property value (or, frequently, the natural logarithm of the measured
 property value) for the ith data point,

 ŷ i = the predicted property value (or, frequently, the natural logarithm of the predicted
 property value) for the ith data point using the model fitted to all n data points,

 y = the average (mean) of the n measured property values (or, frequently, the average of
 the natural logarithm of the measured property values).

 The residual sum of squares (RSS), which would equal zero if the model was perfect (i.e.,
for all n data points, the predicted property value coincides perfectly with the measured value),
can be calculated using Equation 5.1. The RSS can be compared to the total variability in the
regression model, or the total sum of squares (TSS) expressed as the squared difference between
each measured property value yi and the average y :

 Generally R2 statistics take values between 0 and 1. Negative values of R2 estimated on
the validation set or R2Adjusted reflect a poor fitting model, a model that contains more terms than
needed to fit the data, or a model fitted to data with one or more very influential data points.
More than a minor difference between R2 and R2Adjusted indicates that the model may contain
more terms than needed to achieve the same goodness of fit.

 If the fitted model is adequate, the root mean square error (RMSE) provides an estimate
of the standard deviation associated with melting glasses and measuring the associated property
values being modeled. RMSE is therefore expected to be comparable to the measured glass
preparation and property measurement standard deviation assessed from replicates testing. The
statistic RMSE is included as a standard output in the JMP® software (SAS Institute, Version
10.0) used in modeling, and is given by:

 Optimal prediction yields R2Predict closest to 1 but a poor predictive model can yield

 The statistical lack-of-fit (LOF) test checks whether the differences between measured
and predicted property values from a fitted model are larger than expected based on the
experimental and measurement uncertainty in the data. If the predicted versus measured
differences are larger than data uncertainty at a high enough statistical confidence (e.g., greater
than 90%), the model is said to have a statistically significant LOF. Replicate data points
containing all applicable sources of experimental and measurement uncertainty are required to
perform statistical LOF tests.

This process is conducted using a LOF F-test given by:

 The scatter among the replicates of the measured property (multiple yi for a single xi) is
calculated to assess the scatter of points around the best fit curve. The scatter around the
replicates is pooled into the sum of squares of pure error (SSPE). The sum of square error (SSE)
is calculated for all of the model data points. The degree of freedom for SSE is np; and the

data points in the kth replicate set, k = 1, 2, …, K.

 The p-value of the F-test (F probability distribution) is used to assess whether the fitted
model has a statistically significant LOF for the modeling dataset. The smaller the LOF p-value,
the greater the indication that the model is inadequate. A threshold LOF p-value of 0.05 is
traditionally used to assess whether the fitted model has a statistically significant LOF
(significant at the 95% confidence level) for the modeling dataset and that a different model
might be required.

5.1 PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na Models

 5.1.1 PCT Dataset

 The combined WTP/ORP dataset contains 524 glasses with data on PCT boron (PCT-B)
and sodium (PCT-Na) releases. For PCT lithium (PCT-Li) release, the number is 484 (Table
4.2). During the development of WTP baseline models, an assessment of the PCT data
determined that 4 glasses were outliers in the PCT-B set; they were subsequently removed before
development of the PCT-B models [2]. The corresponding numbers of outliers removed were 3
and 2, respectively, in developing the WTP PCT-Li and PCT-Na models. Screening of the ORP
data for outliers was not performed for development of the Phase 1 models or in the present
work. Consequently, the numbers of HLW glasses available for the Phase 1 PCT models are 520
(PCT-B), 481 (PCT-Li), and 522 (PCT-Na) (see Table 5.1). When all of the glasses are divided
into two groups according to alumina concentrations, Table 5.1 shows that considerably more
PCT data were gathered for the low alumina glasses (Group 1, Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%). Data
collected for the high-alumina (HLW14) and ultra-high-alumina (HLW14-UH) matrices
modestly improved the situation but further augmentation is required to provide better and more
even coverage in the Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) compositional space. All available data were
used to revise the global PCT models, as discussed below, while the Group 1 and Group 2 data
were used respectively to support the revisions of the Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%)and Local 2
(Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) models.

 Section 4 summarizes the new PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na data collected for the
ORP-2014 glasses. Figure 4.1 shows that both the ranges and distribution of PCT releases are
very similar among the ORP glasses. The PCT-release ranges for the ORP glasses are narrower
than those for the WTP glasses. There is a general lack of data with large PCT releases among all
glasses and model development could benefit from more data with PCT releases above the
respective contract release limits.

 35

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

 5.1.2 Development of PCT Models

 Four Phase 1 models were previously developed for the PCT release of each of the three
elements (i.e., B, Li, and Na) [23]. They included a global model that employed all available
glasses in the modeling dataset and one Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and two Local 2 (Al2O3 >
13.1 wt%) models. Development of the Phase 1 PCT models closely followed the WTP model
development approach and made use of the baseline models as starting points [2]. Specifically,
based on component distributions of the HLW glasses, the WTP development effort determined
that 24 mixture components have sufficient ranges and distribution to support model terms.
These were Al2O3, B2O3, CaO, CdO, Cr2O3, Fe2O3, K2O, La2O3, Li2O, MgO, MnO, Na2O, NiO,
PbO, Sb2O3, SeO2, SiO2, SrO, ThO2, TiO2, Tl2O, UO3, ZnO, and ZrO2. These oxide components
and “Others” (the sum of all remaining components in glass) were used in initial PCT modeling.
The final recommended WTP baseline PCT models were all 18-term partial quadratic mixture
(PQM) models with bias correction (for a total of 19 terms) on the natural logarithms of the PCT
releases. The Phase 1 modeling efforts considered only PQM models using natural logarithmic
transformations of PCT normalized releases with the following model form but no bias
correction:

where ln(CB), ln(CLi), and ln(CNa) are the natural logarithms of normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and

 q
such that  xi  1 , and the bi, bii and bij are coefficients to be estimated. Modeling began by
 i 1
fitting the PCT data to a first-order model with no quadratic terms. The fitting results determined
the selection of linear modeling terms in the following manner. The 99%-confidence intervals of
the model parameter estimates for the linear terms were compared against the mean response. If
the mean response fell within the confidence levels, it was an indication that the property was not
significantly affected by the model term [47, 48], which was then eliminated from the model.
This approach was similar to that adopted in the development of LAW property-composition
models [49]. Components that were determined to be of insufficient significance were included
in “Others”. The nonlinear terms in the components of the PQM model were then evaluated
using JMP® Response Surface methodologies2 on the full dataset. The Response Surface (RS)
method was employed in order to identify both the significant cross-product and square terms.
Stepwise regression allowed selection of the significant nonlinear terms based on the p-values
calculated in the Student t-tests3. The regression continued until all nonlinear terms in the model

2
  JMP offers two Response Surface Methodologies providing methods of augmenting a linear mixture model with
quadratic terms or other nonlinear blending terms. The Mixture Response Surface (MRS) model suppresses the
intercept, includes all the linear main-effect terms, excludes all the square terms (such as X1×X1), but includes all
cross terms (such as X1×X2). The Response Surface (RS) is different from MRS only by including all square terms
(X1×X1).
3
  The t-test was used in a stepwise regression process to identify important cross-terms in a model while linear terms
were selected on the basis of their confidence intervals. The mixed direction (alternating forward and backward
steps) approach was selected. The t-test computes a t-statistic equal to a model coefficient divided by its standard
deviation. The t-statistic is then compared to the t probability distribution to determine the probability of getting a

 36

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

had p-values lower than 0.05. In the current revision of the Phase 1 models, the same fitting
routine was followed but with the expanded data set to provide the Phase 2 models.

 In Phase 1 and the present work, k-fold cross validation was used for the PCT models. In
k-fold validation, JMP® splits the dataset into k subsets. Each of the subsets is used to validate
the model obtained by fitting with the rest of the data, giving a total of k models and validations.
This approach allows maximum use of the available data while maintaining a validation set.
Model development was performed with k = 5.

 Two local models were developed for Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) glasses during
Phase 1 and they were designated as the Local 2a and Local 2b models. The Local 2a models
were developed using the approach described above for the Group 2 glass data. The Local 2b
models were developed using the linear model terms from the global model. Consequently, the
Local 2b models generally included more modeling terms than the Local 2a models. Although
employing additional modeling parameters in the Local 2b model may not be statistically
appropriate and may lead to over-fitting, it can be used to determine how the predictive
performance of the local model is impacted by including terms from the global model.

 5.1.3 Phase 2 PCT Models

PCT-B Models

 The Phase 1 global model for ln(PCT-B) release developed for 441 HLW glasses was a
22-term PQM model [23]. Table 5.2 presents the coefficients of the global model updated with
520 HLW glasses. Table 5.1 also contains the coefficient standard deviations, p-values for the
quadratic terms, and performance statistics for the modeling and k-fold validation. The Phase 1
global model is also given in Table 5.2 for comparison. The revised model is a 29-term PQM
model with 12 linear terms and 17 non-linear terms. Compared with the Phase 1 model, the
updated Phase 2 model includes 2 more linear terms and 5 more quadratic terms. The new linear
terms are CaO and ZrO2.

       The model assessment statistics R2 = 0.801, R2A = 0.789, R2P = 0.769, and RMSE = 0.417
for the Phase 2 model are somewhat better than the corresponding statistics for the Phase 1
model. They are also generally comparable to or better than the WTP baseline model. It should
be noted, however, that no bias correction was applied in the Phase 1 or revised model. Figure
5.1 displays the predicted versus measured plot for 520 glasses for the revised global PCT-B
model. Different symbols are used in Figure 5.1 to distinguish Group 1 glasses (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1
wt%) from the Group 2 glasses (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Figure 5.1 suggests that both glass groups
have similar PCT-B release ranges, unlike the Phase 1 distribution where most of the glasses

t-statistic at least as large as a pre-determined limit. The resulting probability is referred to as a p-value and
represents the probability of incorrectly deciding a coefficient is significantly different than zero. JMP outputs
estimated model coefficients, coefficients standard errors, t-statistics, and p-values. The smaller the p-value the
stronger is the evidence that the coefficient is significantly different from zero, and that the corresponding model
term is needed. To avoid running models with too many potentially unnecessary terms, JMP evaluates p-values for
the coefficients in a “full” model, and the model term whose coefficient is statistically insignificant is not included.

with high PCT-B release are Group 1 glasses [23]. As noted previously, the effect of Al2O3 on

Phase 2 model tends to under-predict PCT-B release above approximately 0.50 ln(g/l).

 Table 5.3 summarizes and compares the Phase 1 and Phase 2 Local 1 PCT-B models
developed for the HLW glasses. The Phase 2 Local 1 model is a 30-term PQM based on a
modeling set of 402 HLW glasses with Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%. The modeling and the k-fold
validation R2 statistics are slightly worse than those for the global model. The Local 1 model
incorporates 2 additional linear terms (Bi2O3 and CaO). The modeling statistics are comparable
between the two models, which is expected since the number of new glasses added during Phase
2 is relatively modest (32 for a total glass count of 402). Figure 5.2 displays the predicted versus
