 The number of Group 2 HLW glasses with PCT-B data available for modeling has grown
from 71 in Phase 1 to 118 in Phase 2. The Local 2a models from the 2 phases are given in Table
5.4. The Phase 1 Local 2a model is an 11-term PQM model with only six linear mixture model
terms while the Phase 2 model is a 15-term PQM model with eight linear terms (including Al2O3
and CaO). Both the goodness-of-fit and k-fold validation statistics show significant improvement
over those for the Phase 1 Local 2a (and Phase 2 global) model.

 Another local model was developed for the 118 Group 2 glasses using the linear model
terms from the global model. This model is identified as Local 2b (Table 5.5). Table 5.5 also
gives the Phase 1 Local 2b model for comparison. As discussed above, while employing
additional modeling parameters in the Local 2b model (12 linear terms vs. 8 linear terms for the
Local 2a model) may not be statistically appropriate and may lead to over-fitting, it can be useful
to provide insights on how the predictive performance of the local model is impacted by
including terms from the global model. The modeling statistics in Table 5.5 show improvement
from those for the Local 2a model (and the Phase 1 Local 2b model). Figure 5.3 shows the
predicted versus measured plots for both Local 2 PCT-B models and compares them with the
predictions made with the global model.

PCT-Li Models

 Modeling results for PCT-Li release are summarized in Tables 5.6 through 5.9. Table 5.6
presents the coefficients of the revised global (34-term PQM) model for ln(PCT-Li) release
developed for 481 HLW glasses. The table also contains the coefficient standard deviations,
p-values for the quadratic terms, and performance statistics for the modeling and k-fold
validation. The Phase 1 global PCT-Li model, developed with data from 412 HLW glasses, is
included in Table 5.6 for comparison. The Phase 2 global model consists of 11 linear and 23
quadratic terms. Compared with the Phase 1 model, the updated model incorporates one more
linear model term and six more non-linear terms. The linear term added in the Phase 2 model is
CaO.

 38

 The model assessment statistics for the updated global PCT-Li model are slightly better
than the corresponding statistics for the Phase 1 model. Both models do not include bias
correction, as was applied in the WTP baseline HLW model. The RMSE is comparable to the
Phase 1 model value, suggesting the global model has a significant LOF (replicate standard
deviation results are described in the Phase 1 report [23]). Figure 5.4 displays the predicted
versus measured plot for 481 glasses for the Phase 1 global PCT-Li model. Different symbols are
used to distinguish Group 1 glasses (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) from the Group 2 glasses (Al2O3
> 13.1 wt%). Figure 5.4 suggests that both glass groups have similar PCT-Li release ranges,
unlike the Phase 1 distribution where most of the glasses with high PCT-Li release are Group 1

PCT-Li release above approximately 0.5 ln(g/l).

 Table 5.7 summarizes the Local 1 PCT-Li models developed for the HLW glasses with
Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt% from both phases. The Phase 2 model is a PQM model with 28 terms (12
linear terms) based on a modeling set of 369 HLW glasses, which is slightly larger than the
Phase 1 modeling set. The modeling statistics are hence comparable to (or slightly worse than)
those for the Phase 1 model. Both the global and Local 1 models have the same set of linear
model terms except for Bi2O3, which is not included in the Phase 1 model. Figure 5.5 displays
the predicted versus measured plot for the Phase 2 Local 1 PCT-Li model and compares them
with the predictions made with the Phase 2 global model. Under-prediction of the Local 1 model
at PCT-Li release above approximately 0.50 ln(g/l) is observed.

 The number of Group 2 HLW glasses with PCT-Li data available for modeling is 112,
which represents an expansion over the Phase 1 modeling set (69). The resulting models, Local
2a and Local 2b, are given in Tables 5.8 and 5.9, respectively. Local 2a is a 13-term PQM model
with 8 linear mixture terms, 2 more than the Phase 1 model (Al2O3 and CaO). Inclusion of Al2O3
after significance testing of model terms suggests that the Phase 2 modeling set provides a better
compositional coverage to allow prediction of the Al2O3 effects on PCT-Li. Nevertheless, the
Group 2 glass data set is still relatively limited (e.g., the WTP baseline dataset consists of 250
glasses). Figure 5.6 shows the predicted versus measured plots for both of the Local 2 PCT-Li
models and compares them with the predictions made with the Phase 2 global model. Under-
prediction by the Local 2 models is not as noticeable as in the global and Local 1 models. There
is, however, a relative lack of data in the high PCT-Li release region (i.e., > 1.0 ln(g/l)). The
goodness-of-fit and k-fold validation statistics for both Phase 2 Local 2 models are considerably
better than those for the respective Phase 1 models.

5.2 Spinel T1% Models

 5.2.1 Spinel T1% Dataset

 The total number of HLW glasses available for spinel T1% modeling during Phase 1 was
340 [23]. The newly collected data described in Section 2 increased this number to 392. This
represents only a modest expansion, as many of the matrix glasses did not show enough
crystallization upon heat treatment to allow estimation of spinel T1% values. In addition, many of

 40

the actively designed ORP-2014 glasses precipitated non-spinel phases since they were
formulated for wastes high in zirconium and strontium. Table 5.1 provides a summary of the
number of HLW glasses available for spinel T1% modeling, including division of the glasses
according to their aluminum concentrations into Group 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Group 2
(Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Table 5.1 shows that there are many more glasses (277) that populate the
compositional region of Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%, with all of the Group 2 glasses (115) coming from
the ORP dataset. Moreover, as described above, since most of the statistically designed glasses
belong in Group 1, the distribution of the Group 1 glasses in the compositional region is
expected to be more even than that of Group 2.

 5.2.2 Spinel T1% Model Development

 Four Phase 1 models were previously developed for predicting spinel T1% [23]. They
included a global model that employed all available glasses in the modeling dataset and one
Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and two Local 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) models. Development of the
spinel T1% models closely followed the WTP model development approach and made use of the
baseline model as a starting point [2]. The WTP modeling effort considered different model
forms for the composition dependence of spinel T1% including linear mixture (LM), PQM, and
partial cubic mixture (PCM) models, with the final recommended WTP baseline spinel T1%
model being a 20-term PQM model [2].

 The present revision of the Phase 1 model followed the same approach as in Phase 1
model development and considered only PQM models. The specific PQM model form is given
by

 i

 The modeling approach was similar to that used for PCT model development, where
comparison of the mean response value (963.6 °C for 392 glasses) versus the 99%-confidence
intervals of the model parameter estimates was used to select the model linear terms. The
nonlinear terms in the components of the PCM model were then evaluated using JMP®
Response Surface methodologies on the full dataset. The Response Surface (RS) method was
employed in order to identify both the significant cross-product and square terms. Stepwise
regression allowed selection of the significant nonlinear terms based on the p-values calculated
in the Student t-tests. The regression continued until all nonlinear terms in the model had
p-values lower than 0.05. In the current revision of the Phase 1 models, the same fitting approach
was followed but with the expanded data set to provide the Phase 2 models.

 41

 In Phase 1 and the present work, k-fold cross validation was used for the spinel T1%
models. In k-fold validation, JMP® splits the dataset into k subsets. Each of the subsets is used to
validate the model obtained by fitting with the rest of the data, giving a total of k models and
validations. This approach allows maximum use of the available data while maintaining a
validation set. Model development was performed with k = 5.

 Two local models were developed for Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) glasses during
Phase 1 and they were designated as the Local 2a and Local 2b models. The Local 2a models
were developed using the approach described above for the Group 2 glass data. The Local 2b
models were developed using the linear model terms from the global model. Consequently, the
Local 2b models generally included more modeling terms than the Local 2a models. Although
employing additional modeling parameters in the Local 2b model may not be statistically
appropriate and may lead to over-fitting, it can be used to determine how the predictive
performance of the local model is impacted by including terms from the global model.

 5.2.3 Phase 2 Spinel T1% Models

 The Phase 1 global model for spinel T1% is a 30-term PQM model with 14 quadratic
terms. It was developed for 340 combined WTP/ORP glasses and covered a much wider
compositional range when compared to the WTP baseline model. Its modeling statistics are
slightly worse than those for the WTP baseline model, although both have similar RMSE. The
RMSE is an estimate of the uncertainty in spinel T1% values for the HLW glasses if the model
does not have statistically significant LOF. The WTP baseline model has a RMSE value of 45.9
while the Phase 1 global model has a RMSE value of 52.3. Both values are larger than the
standard deviation from replicate testing [23], indicating there is substantial LOF in both global
models. In addition, the WTP baseline model did not provide satisfactory prediction for the ORP
glasses with high Al2O3 and Bi2O3 concentrations [23].

 Table 5.14 presents the coefficients of the Phase 2 global (28-term PQM) model for
spinel T1% developed for 392 HLW glasses. The table also contains the coefficient standard
deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and performance statistics for the modeling and
k-fold validation. The Phase 1global model for spinel T1%, developed with data from 340 HLW
glasses, is included in Table 5.14 for comparison. The Phase 2 global model consists of 16 linear
and 12 quadratic terms. Compared with the Phase 1 global model, the Phase 2 model
incorporates two fewer non-linear terms.

        The Phase 2 model statistics of R2 = 0.815, R2A = 0.801, R2P = 0.780, and RMSE =
54.395 for the global model are worse than the corresponding statistics for the Phase 1 model.
The k-fold validation R2 of 0.773 can be compared with the Phase 1 value of 0.807. The RMSE
is an estimate of the uncertainty in spinel T1% values for the HLW glasses if the model does not
have statistically significant LOF. The RMSE = 54.395 (52.313 in Phase 1) for the Phase 2
model is considerably larger than the replicate standard deviation (SD) = 26.45 discussed in the
Phase 1 report [23], indicating the global model has a substantial LOF. Therefore, all spinel T1%
models, including the WTP baseline model and those developed using the combined WTP/ORP
dataset, are subject to relatively large prediction uncertainties. Figure 5.10 displays the predicted

 42

versus measured plot for 392 glasses for the Phase 2 global spinel T1% model. Different symbols
are used in Figure 5.10 to distinguish Group 1 glasses (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) from the Group 2
glasses (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Figure 5.10 shows that both glass groups have similarly large
scatter, resulting from the relatively large uncertainties in the estimated spinel T1% values and the
significant LOF of the models.

 Table 5.15 summarizes the Local 1 spinel T1% models developed for the HLW glasses
with Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt% from both phases. The Phase 2 model is a PQM model with 35 terms (16
linear terms) based on a modeling set of 277 HLW glasses, which has 22 more glasses than the
Phase 1 modeling set. The modeling statistics are slightly worse than for those from Phase 1.
Both the global and Local 1 models have the same set of linear model terms. Figure 5.11
displays the predicted versus measured plot for the Phase 2 Local 1 spinel T1% model and
compares them with the predictions made with the Phase 2 global model.

 The number of Group 2 HLW glasses with spinel T1% data available for modeling is 115,
which represents a modest expansion over the Phase 1 modeling set (92). The resulting models,
Local 2a and Local 2b, are given in Tables 5.16 and 5.17, respectively. Local 2a is an 18-term
PQM model with 11 linear mixture terms, 2 more than the Phase 1 model (Cr2O3 and Fe2O3),
both of which are important components in spinel formation. Their inclusion in the model after
significance testing of model terms suggests that the Phase 2 modeling set provides better
compositional coverage to allow prediction of their effects on spinel T1%. Additionally, the
goodness-of-fit statistics are considerably improved compared to those from Phase 1.
Nevertheless, the Group 2 glass data set is still relatively limited, especially since the HLW14
and HLW14-UH matrices resulted in only 20 estimated spinel T1% values (out of 45 glasses).
Figure 5.12 shows the predicted versus measured plots for both Local 2 spinel T1% models and
compares them with the predictions made with the Phase 2 global model. As observed in Figures
5.10 and 5.11 for the global and Local 1 models, Figure 5.12 shows that the Local 2 models have
relatively large scatter.

5.3 Electrical Conductivity Models

 5.3.1 Electrical Conductivity Dataset

 Of the ORP-2014 glasses collected and described in Sections 2 and 4, 70 glasses have
data on EC (Table 4.1). The dataset used in the development of the Phase 1 global EC model
consisted of 374 HLW glasses, all of which have EC data for at least four different temperatures.
The total number of HLW glasses available now for EC modeling is 444 (all glasses with data at
four different temperatures) and 1776 data points. Table 5.1 provides a summary of the number
of HLW glasses available for modeling, including the division of the glasses according to
aluminum concentrations into Group 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%).
Table 5.1 shows that there are considerably more glasses (329) that populate the compositional
region of Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%, with all of the Group 2 glasses (115) coming from the ORP dataset
(maximum Al2O3 = 29.6 wt%). Moreover, as described above, since most of the statistically
designed glasses belong in Group 1, coverage of the Group 1 compositional region is expected to
be more even than that of Group 2.

 43

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

 Section 4.3 summarizes the EC data collected for the ORP-2014 glasses. The WTP
requirement for HLW glass melt EC is that it be between 0.1 to 0.7 S/cm in the temperature
range of 1100 to 1200 °C. For measurements taken in this temperature range, only 4 are outside
of this range. The measured EC values for 444 HLW glasses included in the modeling dataset
range from 0.014 to 1.376 S/cm.

 5.3.2 Electrical Conductivity Model Development

 Four Phase 1 models were previously developed for predicting EC of glass melts [23].
They included a global model that employed all available glasses in the modeling dataset and
one Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and two Local 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) models. The performance
of the Phase 1 global EC model is slightly better than that of the WTP baseline model based on
modeling statistics, in spite of a greatly expanded composition region. Thus, it is likely that local
models may not offer significant advantages and improvements over a global model for EC
prediction. Therefore only global models were considered in the present work.

 Development of the Phase 1 global EC model closely followed the WTP model
development approach and made use of the baseline model as a starting point [2]. The WTP
baseline model development work examined various methods in developing, evaluating, and
validating property-composition models. Different model forms for the temperature and
composition dependence of EC were considered, with the final recommended WTP baseline EC
model being a truncated T2 PQM model [2] on the natural logarithm of the EC.

 The present revision of the Phase 1 model followed the same approach as in Phase 1
model development and considered only the truncated T2 equation expanded as a PQM model.
Similarly, as was the case with the WTP baseline modeling, natural logarithmic transformations
of EC were used in modeling. The advantages of using logarithmic transformations in modeling
have been discussed previously [2]. The model form for the EC model is given by

 q
 q q 1 q
  q xi

 2
 , (5.11)
                           i 1                i 1      i   j            i 1 (T / 1000 )

where ln(EC) denotes the natural logarithm of EC (in S/cm), the xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) are the
 q
normalized mass fractions of the q glass oxides such that  xi  1 , ai, aii, aij, and bi are
 i 1
coefficients to be estimated, and T is the temperature (in Kelvin).

 The modeling approach was similar to that described above for PCT model development,
where comparison of the mean response value (-1.383 for 444 glasses and 1776 data points)
versus the 99%-confidence intervals of the model parameter estimates was used to select the
model linear terms. The nonlinear terms in the components of the PCM model were then
evaluated using JMP® Response Surface methodologies on the full dataset. The Response
Surface (RS) method was employed in order to identify both the significant cross-product and

 44

square terms. Stepwise regression allowed selection of the significant nonlinear terms based on
the p-values calculated in the Student t-tests. The regression continued until all nonlinear terms
in the model had p-values lower than 0.0001 (in order to minimize the number of model terms).
Instead of data-splitting, k-fold cross validation was used for the EC models developed, with
k = 5.

 5.3.3 Phase 2 Electrical Conductivity Model

 The Phase 1 global model to predict EC is a 25-term reduced truncated T2 linear mixture
model with 8 linear terms and 17 cross terms. It provides a slightly improved fit to the EC data
for the WTP/ORP glasses when compared to the baseline WTP model based on modeling
statistics, in spite of a much expanded compositional region [23].

 Table 5.18 presents the coefficients of the Phase 2 global (22-term truncated T2) model
for EC developed for 444 HLW glasses. The table also contains the coefficient standard
deviations, p-values for the cross terms, and performance statistics for the modeling and k-fold
validation. The Phase 1 global EC model, developed with data from 374 HLW glasses, is
included in Table 5.18 for comparison. The Phase 2 global model consists of 9 linear and 13
cross terms. Compared with the Phase 1 model, the updated model incorporates 1 additional
linear model term (ZrO2) but 4 fewer non-linear terms.

        The model assessment statistics R2 = 0.932, R2A = 0.931, R2P = 0.930 and RMSE = 0.174
for the Phase 2 model all show slight improvement over the corresponding statistics for the
Phase 1 model. The k-fold validation R2 is 0.930. However, the RMSE is larger than the pooled
uncertainty of ln(S/cm) described in Phase 1 [23], suggesting the global model has a significant
LOF. Figure 5.13 displays the predicted versus measured plot for 444 glasses for the Phase 2
global EC model. Different symbols are used in Figure 5.13 to distinguish Group 1 glasses
(Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) from the Group 2 glasses (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Figure 5.13 shows that the
scatter is larger for Group 1 glasses. The plot shows that the two glasses HLW07-28 and
HLW07-40 are outliers; they were also found to be the primary sources of outlying observations
during WTP model development.

5.4 Viscosity Models

 5.4.1 Viscosity Dataset

 Of the ORP-2014 glasses collected and described in Sections 2 and 4, 66 glasses have
data on viscosity (Table 4.1). The dataset used in the development of the Phase 1 global viscosity
model consisted of 345 HLW glasses, all of which have viscosity data for at least four different
temperatures. The total number of HLW glasses available now for viscosity modeling is 411 (all
glasses with data at four different temperatures) and 1644 data points. Table 5.1 provides a
summary of the number of HLW glasses available for modeling, including the division of the
glasses according to aluminum concentrations into Group 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Group 2
(Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Table 5.1 shows that there are considerably more glasses (302) that

 45

The Catholic University of America Enhanced HLW Glass Property-Composition Models - Phase 2
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-15R3400-1, Rev. 0

populate the compositional region of Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%, with all of the Group 2 glasses (109)
coming from the ORP dataset (maximum Al2O3 = 29.6 wt% in HLW14-UH18). Moreover, as
described above, since most of the statistically designed glasses belong to Group 1, coverage of
the Group 1 compositional region is expected to be more even than that of Group 2.

 Section 4.4 summarizes the viscosity data collected for the ORP-2014 glasses. The WTP
requirement for HLW glass melt viscosity is that it be within the limits of 10 to 150 poise at
1100 °C. The measured viscosity values for 411 HLW glasses included in the modeling dataset
range from 3.6 to 4617 P (measurement temperature range of 931 to 1282 °C).

 5.4.2 Viscosity Model Development

 Four Phase 1 models were previously developed for predicting the viscosity of glass
melts [23]. They included a global model that employed all available glasses in the modeling
dataset and one Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and two Local 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) models. The
performance of the Phase 1 global viscosity model is very comparable to that of the WTP
baseline model, in spite of a greatly expanded composition region. Thus, it is likely that local
models may not offer significant advantages and improvements over global models for viscosity
prediction. Therefore only global models were considered in the present work.

 Development of the Phase 1 global viscosity model closely followed the WTP model
development approach and made use of the baseline model as the starting point [2]. The WTP
baseline model development work examined various methods in developing, evaluating, and
validating property-composition models. Different model forms for the temperature and
composition dependence of viscosity were considered, with the final recommended WTP
baseline viscosity model being a truncated T2 PQM model [2] on the natural logarithm of the
viscosity.

 The present revision of the Phase 1 model followed the same approach as in Phase 1
model development and considered only the truncated T2 equation expanded as a PQM model.
Similarly, as was the case with the WTP baseline modeling, natural logarithmic transformations
of viscosity were used in modeling. The advantages of using logarithmic transformations in
modeling have been discussed previously [2]. The model form for the viscosity model is given
by

 q

  q xi

 , (5.12)
                          i 1                  i 1         i  j             i 1 (T / 1000)

where ln(η) denotes the natural logarithm of viscosity (in P), the xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) are the
 q
normalized mass fractions of the q glass oxide such that  xi  1 , ai, aii, aij, and bi are
 i 1
coefficients to be estimated, and T is the temperature (in Kelvin).

 46

 The modeling approach is similar to that described above for PCT model development,
where comparison of the mean response value (4.285 for 411 glasses and 1644 data points)
versus the 99%-confidence intervals of the model parameter estimates was used to select the
model linear terms. The nonlinear terms in the components of the PCM model were then
evaluated using JMP® Response Surface methodologies on the full dataset. The Response
Surface (RS) method was employed in order to identify both the significant cross-product and
square terms. Stepwise regression allowed selection of the significant nonlinear terms based on
the p-values calculated in the Student t-tests. The regression continued until all nonlinear terms
in the model had p-values lower than 0.0001 (in order to minimize the number of model terms).
Instead of data-splitting, k-fold cross validation was used for the viscosity models developed,
with k = 5.

 5.4.3 Phase 2 Viscosity Model

 The Phase 1 global model to predict melt viscosity is a 29-term reduced truncated T2
linear mixture model with 10 linear terms and 19 cross terms. It provides a comparable fit to the
viscosity data for the WTP/ORP glasses when compared to the baseline WTP model based on
modeling statistics, in spite of a much expanded compositional region [23].

 Table 5.19 presents the coefficients of the Phase 2 global (30-term truncated T2) model
for viscosity developed for 411 HLW glasses. The table also contains the coefficient standard
deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and performance statistics for modeling and k-fold
validation. The Phase 1 global viscosity model, developed with data from 345 HLW glasses, is
included in Table 5.19 for comparison. The Phase 2 global model consists of 10 linear and 20
cross terms. Compared with the Phase 1 model, the revised model incorporates the same set of
linear model term but one more non-linear term.

        The model assessment statistics R2 = 0.980, R2A = 0.980, R2P = 0.979 and RMSE = 0.191
for the Phase 2 model are all similar to the corresponding statistics for the Phase 1 model. So is
the k-fold validation R2 value of 0.930. The RMSE is similar to the Phase 1 RMSE; both are
larger than the pooled uncertainty of ln(P) described in Phase 1 [23], suggesting the global model
has a significant LOF. Figure 5.14 displays the predicted versus measured plot for 444 glasses
for the Phase 2 global viscosity model. Different symbols are used in Figure 5.13 to distinguish
Group 1 glasses (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) from the Group 2 glasses (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Figure 5.14
shows that the scatter in the data is similar for both glass groups.

5.5 TCLP Cd-Release Models

 5.5.1 TCLP Cd-Release Dataset

 Of the HLW glasses collected and described in Sections 2 and 4, 27 glasses have data on
TCLP Cd-release (excluding non-detects, see Table 4.8). This represents only a modest
expansion of the TCLP Cd-release dataset. Along with the Phase 1 data, the total number of
combined WTP/ORP HLW glasses available for TCLP Cd-modeling is 184. Table 5.1 provides a

 47

summary of the number of HLW glasses available for modeling, including the division of the
glasses according to aluminum concentrations into Group 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Group 2
(Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Table 5.1 shows that there are considerably more glasses (132) that
populate the compositional region of Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%, with all of the Group 2 glasses (52)
coming from the ORP dataset (maximum Al2O3 = 28.7 wt%). Moreover, as described above,
since most of the statistically designed glasses belong to Group 1, coverage of the Group 1
compositional region is expected to be more even than that of Group 2.

 Section 4.5 summarizes the ORP-2014 TCLP-Cd data collected. The ORP-2014 glasses
did not expand the range Cd-releases of HLW glasses and the range of unnormalized Cd-releases
of all HLW glasses remained the same as before: between 0.007 to 2.040 mg/l for the 184 HLW
glasses in the modeling set, while the normalized Cd-releases range from 0.014 to 0.0422 g/l.

 5.5.2 TCLP Cd-Release Model Development

 The WTP baseline model development work examined various methods in developing,
evaluating, and validating property-composition models [1]. Three different model forms for the
composition dependence of Cd-releases were considered. In addition, in order to support
delisting compliance, two different laboratories (STL-Knoxville and STL-St. Louis) performed
the TCLP testing on different subsets of the glasses, producing results with different
uncertainties. It was consequently decided that modeling would be performed via weighted least
squares (WLS) regression based on the different standard deviations obtained.

 During Phase model development, WLS regression was not considered for TCLP Cd-
release models because replicate TCLP testing at VSL had not yet been performed and therefore
comparable data on uncertainties associated with this data set were not available. Moreover, the
generalized normalization approach of adding a ln(mCdO) term into the model, which was found
to be beneficial for the WTP TCLP model [1], was not adopted since it did not improve the fit
compared to ordinary normalization of the TCLP Cd-release. Thus, the only model form
considered in Phase 1 was a PQM model given by

 q
 q q 1 q
 

                                       i 1                i 1       i  j         

where ln(rCd) denotes the natural logarithm of normalized TCLP Cd-release in g/l, the xi (i = 1, 2,
 q
…, q) are the normalized mass fractions of the q glass oxide such that  xi  1 , bi, bii, are bij are

coefficients to be estimated.

 The present modeling approach was identical to that from Phase 1 and the only model
form considered was Equation 5.12. Also, local models were not investigated for TCLP
Cd-release. The linear terms in the model were selected by comparing the mean response value
(-3.054 for 184 glasses) to the 99%-confidence intervals of the model parameter estimates. The
nonlinear terms in the components of the PCM model were then evaluated using JMP®

 48

Response Surface methodologies on the full dataset. The Response Surface (RS) method was
employed in order to identify both the important cross-product and square terms. Stepwise
regression allowed the selection of the significant nonlinear terms based on the p-values
calculated in the Student t-tests. The regression continued until all nonlinear terms in the model
had p-values lower than 0.05. Instead of data-splitting, k-fold cross validation was used for the
TCLP Cd-release models developed, with k = 5.

 5.5.3 Phase 2 TCLP Cd-Release Models

 The Phase 1 global model to predict TCLP Cd-release is a 25-term PQM model that
consists of 14 linear and 11 quadratic terms. As noted above, the Phase 1 model form is different
from that of the WTP baseline model. The Phase 1 model covers a much wider glass
compositional region than the WTP model but its model assessment statistics are worse than
those determined for the WTP model [23].

 Table 5.20 presents the coefficients of the Phase 2 global (25-term PQM) model for
predicting ln(rCd) values developed for 184 HLW glasses. The table also contains the coefficient
standard deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and performance statistics for the modeling
and k-fold validation. The Phase 1 TCLP Cd model, developed with data from 157 HLW glasses,
is included in Table 5.20 for comparison. Both models have the same set of linear terms but the
Phase 2 model includes 5 fewer quadratic terms.

 The Phase 2 model assessment statistics R2 = 0.874, R2A = 0.859, R2P = 0.832, and
RMSE = 0.277 are all somewhat worse than the corresponding statistic for the Phase 1 model.
Figure 5.16 displays the predicted versus measured plot for all 184 glasses using the Phase 2
global TCLP Cd-release model. Different symbols are used in Figure 5.15 to distinguish Group 1
glasses (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) from the Group 2 glasses (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). Figure 5.16 shows
that the high-alumina glasses generally have higher rCd values. The scatter is roughly comparable
for both glass groups and no obvious bias is observed.

 SECTION 6.0
 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

 As a continuation of the Phase 1 HLW model development work as part of a phased
approach to enhance and expand HLW glass property-composition models, additional data were
collected for the HLW glass dataset and used to revise the models developed during Phase 1.
These data are for glasses that are collectively referred to as ORP-2014 glasses. The sources of
additional data for the Phase 2 work included: (i) actively designed glasses formulated to support
various glass studies at VSL since the completion of Phase 1; and (ii) glasses from two
statistically designed matrices to supplement the existing HLW compositional space. The Phase
2 matrix design was intended to augment and improve the coverage in the high aluminum region.
The design consisted of two parts: a high alumina (13.1 wt% < Al2O3 < 22.1 wt%) region
covered by 23 glasses and a ultra-high alumina (Al2O3 ≥ 22.1 wt%) region covered by 22 glasses.
By contrast, a large majority of the actively designed glasses contained < 13.1 wt% Al2O3 since
they were formulated to support testing of different waste types (e.g., HLW high in zirconium or
sulfur). Overall, there are 108 ORP-2014 glasses (excluding CCC replicates) and 49 of them
have an alumina concentration above 13.1 wt%. The compiled data include glass compositions,
PCT releases, spinel T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP Cd-releases, and nepheline
formation upon CCC. Collection of these data for the ORP-2014 glasses continues to expand the
combined WTP/ORP dataset such that considerably more data are now available to support
property-composition model development than those used to develop the WTP baseline HLW
models (Table 5.1).

 Phase 2 revision of the HLW models involved updating the Phase 1 models using the
same model forms and fitting approach. Global models were developed for all properties and
local models were also evaluated for some properties, as was the case in Phase 1. The updated
global models perform generally very similarly to the Phase 1 global models. The same
conclusion can be drawn for the Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) models, which is likely due in part
to the fact that that modeling set contains all of the glasses used in the development of WTP
baseline models. By contrast, the performance of the Local 2 models appears to benefit from the
addition of new data. This is evident from the improved modeling statistics and the inclusion of
new model terms for oxides that are known to play significant roles in determining the property
value (e.g., Cr2O3 and Fe2O3 were excluded from the Phase 1 spinel T1% Local 2a model but
were determined to be significant in Phase 2). Further, a more even distribution in property
values is seen for the Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) glasses as compared to the Phase 1 data (e.g.,
PCT boron release). Nevertheless, it is expected that development of improved high-alumina
local models will require the addition of still more data.

 Phase 2 models were developed for PCT releases, spinel T1%, electrical conductivity,
viscosity, and TCLP Cd-releases. Models for nepheline formation are the subject of a separate
task. Only the global models for predicting EC and viscosity from Phase 1 were revised using the
new dataset; the local models for these properties were not revised in Phase 2. This followed the

 50

findings from Phase 1, which showed that the regression and validation statistics for these global
models are similar to or better than those for the WTP baseline models, even though they cover
much wider composition ranges, leading to the conclusion that local models may not offer any
significant advantages over the global models for EC and viscosity. The Phase 2 revised EC and
viscosity models show similar or better performance when compared to the respective Phase 1
and WTP baseline models, further suggesting that local models may not be necessary for these
properties. The Phase 2 global models for PCT releases performed somewhat better than the
corresponding Phase 1 models. In contrast, the Phase 2 models for TCLP release and spinel T1%
showed somewhat worse performance as compared to the respective Phase 1 models. All of
these models have statistically significant LOF, suggesting that the models are not able to fully
account for the complicated non-linear blending effects of HLW glass components on these
properties, which may be in part due to limitations in the extent and distribution of the data.

 It is anticipated that the Phase 2 models will be further revised and improved as more
data become available, including those from future statistical designs to cover the identified
compositional gaps. In this regard, it is noted that the ORP-2014 glasses represent only a
relatively modest improvement in filling the identified compositional gaps, especially in the high
alumina region. This is partly because of the extremely large composition space under
consideration, which has resulted from years of studies of high waste loading glasses for many
different waste types [11-21, 26-29], as compared to that for the WTP baseline.

 51

 SECTION 7.0
 QUALITY ASSURANCE

 This work was conducted under a quality assurance program compliant with the
applicable criteria of 10 CFR 830.120; Office of Civilian Waste Management DOE/RW-0333P,
Quality Assurance Requirements and Description (QARD), Revision 20; the American Society
of Mechanical Engineers (ASME) Nuclear Quality Assurance (NQA)-1, 2004; and DOE Order
414.1 C, Quality Assurance. The program is supplemented by a Quality Assurance Project Plan
for ORP work that is conducted at VSL [50]. Test and procedure requirements by which the
testing activities are planned and controlled are also defined in this plan. The program is
supported by VSL standard operating procedures that were used for this work [51].
Requirements of DOE/RW-0333P are applicable to the glass composition and PCT portions of
the work.

 APPENDIX A

 Selection of HLW Glasses to Support
 the Development of ORP Phase 2 Test Matrices

 A-1

 Appendix A

 Selection of HLW Glasses to Support the Development of ORP Phase 2 Test Matrices

 This appendix identifies the high-level waste (HLW) glasses selected to support the
statistical design and development of two Office of River Protection (ORP) Phase 2 test
matrices. Two Phase 2 test matrices were developed and tested to augment existing HLW glass
data to support HLW property-composition modeling. There are general compositional
differences between existing baseline glasses from the Waste Treatment and Immobilization
Plant (WTP) dataset and existing glasses from the ORP dataset because the ORP glasses were
developed for different waste groups and to accommodate significantly higher waste loadings.

 The two Phase 2 test matrices are labeled as HLW14 (high aluminum) and HLW14-UH
(ultra-high aluminum). The matrix HLW14 was statistically designed to fill gaps for glasses with
Al2O3 < 22.1 wt%, while the matrix HLW14-UH was designed to augment and improve the
coverage in the high alumina regions (Al2O3 > 22.1 wt%). The following tables identify the
existing HLW glasses selected to support test matrix development; all are ORP glasses.

 Table A.1. Selection of Glasses to Support Development of Phase 2 HLW14 Test Matrix.
